> European countries

> Spain: Expelled in 1492, with the ban lifted de jure in 1968.  
> Portugal: Expelled in 1497, though the ban was not fully lifted de jure for centuries.  
> Austria: Expelled in 1421.  
> Lithuania: Expelled in 1495.  
> Middle Eastern and North African countries  
> Iraq: Experienced waves of persecution and forced migration, particularly after the 1948 Arab–Israeli War.  
> Egypt: Saw increased anti-Jewish sentiment and expulsion, especially during the Suez Crisis of 1956.  
> Yemen: Expelled in 1679 and again in 2021.  
> Libya: Experienced forced migration and violence, especially after the 1967 pogrom in Tripoli.  
> Other countries: Morocco, Syria, Lebanon, and Iran also saw forced migration of their Jewish populations, particularly in the mid-to-late 20th century.

> If you need money, then you need to convince me to give it to you. One way is by promising to pay me back plus an additional amount. That additional amount is the lender’s “interest” in the loan. >   
> If I loan you my money, I’m taking an opportunity cost because I could be using that for something else. I can’t invest money that I have loaned out to somebody else, so interest protects me from that.   
>   
> Interest also protects people from defaulting or inflation. Lenders face the risk of non-repayment (default) and that inflation will devalue the money by the time it's repaid, so interest covers this risk. Money today is worth more than the same amount in the future so lenders will naturally pay for this time. It's essentially a fee for having immediate access to funds you don't have, or a reward for delaying your own consumption, for example getting a loan to pay for a mortgage on a house now rather than saving up for 10 years to buy it.  
>

8:45 Respectfully, historians don’t support the idea that Islam came from Jews fleeing Roman rule. The Second Temple fell in 70 CE, but Islam began nearly 600 years later in Mecca. Judaism continued as Judaism, while Islam formed separately in 7th-century Arabia, influenced by but not originating from Jewish groups.

Here are the details:
Judaism and Islam are related as Abrahamic religions, but their timelines don’t line up for that theory:

• The Second Temple was destroyed in 70 CE
• Islam began almost 600 years later, in 610 CE, in Mecca
• Early Muslims were mostly pagan Arabs, not Jewish sects
• Jewish communities in Arabia continued practicing Judaism

After 70 CE, Judaism developed into Rabbinic Judaism — it didn’t transform into Islam.
Islam arose later in the distinct social, linguistic, and religious environment of 7th-century Arabia, while interacting with existing Jewish and Christian communities.

So while the religions share themes (monotheism, prophets, law), Islam didn’t originate from a radical Jewish movement. That view isn’t supported by the historical or archaeological record.

Correction, Professor. Jews did not create Islam as a rebellion against the Romans. Saying that Islam was invented in this way implies that it is a man-made religion, which is disrespectful to the nearly two billion Muslims in the world today—around 90% of whom are non-Arab.


Historically, both Jews and Arabs share connected roots as Semitic peoples. In the Abrahamic tradition, they are descendants of Abraham through his two sons: the Jews through Isaac and his son Jacob, and the Arabs through Ishmael. Because of these shared origins, it would not be accurate to claim that Jews simply moved into the desert and created Islam.



You also mentioned that Judaism is a monotheistic religion similar to Islam, in contrast to the polytheistic beliefs that were common among the Romans and Greeks. If Jews had moved into the desert to create a new religion, it would be difficult to explain why much of Arabia at the time practiced polytheism, worshipping multiple deities. In fact, polytheism was widespread in the Arabian Peninsula and was closely tied to the region’s social and economic structures. According to Islamic belief, the mission of the Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him) was to call people back to the worship of one God. If Islam had simply been created by a group of Jews, it raises the question of why polytheism was so widespread in Arabia beforehand, since that would contradict the idea that the region had already been shaped by a strongly monotheistic movement.

TLDR: Regarding the professors statements in 25:47 Atatürk and Modern Turkey Republic is founded by Dönmeh:
All credible historical sources contradict the claim.

The rumor is political, not historical.

> **@imanokguymostofthetime8719** | 👍 9 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kULUM_eB8KI&lc=UgxW_qATs5Vbx7ZoVsR4AaABAg.AQaDiP9HEgDAQaEJ9Roign)  
>
> Too long version:  
> When you take a step back and look at it, it becomes clearer why these rumors about Atatürk exist. His birth in Selanik, the city’s highly cosmopolitan character at the time, the historical presence of the Sabbatian community there, and the fact that his modernizing reforms were seen as unconventional by some, all created a fertile, if fragile, ground for myths to form. Human thinking, especially in politically charged contexts, tends to link complex events to “hidden identities,” because such stories feel explanatory and carry an emotional punch.  
>   
> In Atatürk’s case, this dynamic is at work. The multiethnic nature of Selanik, gaps in family records, and the intense political debates of the early Republic all provided material that could be spun into speculation. Historians, however, make it clear that there is no documentary evidence to support these claims. The records of both his mother’s and father’s families, their regional origins, and their social positions all consistently contradict the idea. Yet the rumor persists because it functions less as history and more as a symbolic story about identity, legitimacy, and belonging—a story that continues to circulate because it is easy to repeat and emotionally compelling, even though it has no basis in fact.  
>   
> Technically:  
> His mother’s side (Zübeyde Hanım):  
> Her family came from Langaza, a rural Muslim-Turkish farming community.  
> Dönme groups were strictly urban, endogamous, and mercantile — completely different from rural households.  
> No Dönme presence existed in Langaza.  
>   
> His father’s side (Ali Rıza Efendi):  
> Archival records trace his family to Konya–Karaman Turkic settlers who moved to the Balkans via Ottoman iskân policies.  
> This matches population registers and known migration patterns.  
> Again, no overlap with Dönme communities.  
>   
> Dönme communities lived in specific Selanik neighborhoods (Karataş, Yenişehir, Kapancı).  
> Atatürk’s family lived outside these areas and followed mainstream Muslim traditions.  
>   
> The Dönme were tightly endogamous (they married almost exclusively within their sects).  
> Neither side of Atatürk’s family fits their social, geographic, or cultural profile.  
>   
> Historians across the board (Mango, Ortaylı, Zürcher, Turan, Akşin, etc.) agree:  
> There is zero archival evidence connecting Atatürk’s parents to Sabbatian groups.

Jacob Frank / Neville Goddard / William Blake.
Blake-Frank-Goddard Connection
A concise breakdown of the "Radical Imagination" lineage we discussed:
1. The Theological Root: Antinomianism
Both Jacob Frank and William Blake challenged the "Moral Law." While Frank sought "Redemption through Sin," Blake sought "Redemption through Imagination," viewing rigid laws as the "stony" constraints of a fallen mind.
2. The Visionary Influence: Jakob Böhme
Böhme’s mystical concept of "Contraries" (Light/Dark, Yes/No) provided the blueprint for Blake’s mythology and shares the radical psychological depth found in Neville Goddard’s later teachings.
3. The Divine Human & Neville Goddard
The most striking parallel is the shift from an external God to an internal one:
Blake: "The Eternal Body of Man is The Imagination, that is, God himself."
Goddard: "God’s name is 'I AM'—your own wonderful human imagination."
The Result: Both argue that by "entering a State" (Blake) or "assuming a feeling" (Goddard), the individual collapses the distance between desire and reality.
4. The "Mundane Shell"
Blake’s term for the material world’s limitations. He taught that one must "break" this shell through Fourfold Vision, moving beyond simple sensory observation to a state of total creative authority.

NOT, OK. Just to clarify a few points for the record: First, the term is Dönme (Sabbatean Convert), not "Domi." Atatürk was not a Dönme. His family were Yörük Turks from Anatolia who were resettled in the Balkans by the Ottoman Empire to establish a Turkish presence there. Being born in Thessaloniki doesn't make him part of the Sabbatean group anymore than living in a city makes you part of every sub-culture there.
In fact, your argument is upside down. While the Republic focused on Turkish identity, the Ottoman system was the one built on converts. The Ottoman bureaucracy, the Janissaries (Yeniceri), and nearly all the Valide Sultans (Sultans' mothers) were of "Devshirme" (general name for Convert) origin. If you want to talk about a system run by converts, look at the Ottoman Palace, not the Republic.
This "Dönme" narrative you're using is a well-known political tactic used by those who oppose the Republic of Turkey and secular nationalism in an attempt to glorify and bring back the old Ottoman system. Your argument (25:40) is based on political bias, not historical reality.

### **@tminusmat** | 👍 11 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kULUM_eB8KI&lc=UgwHE4IDHQy6WlJZe154AaABAg)

I love Jiang’s historical panorama, but I think he’s accidentally describing something closer to neurotransmission than geopolitical chess.

Imagine civilization as a giant, overcaffeinated nervous system. Money isn’t wealth, it’s dopamine with a logo. Debt is cortisol politely wearing a tie. Religions are long-term memory files that never quite finish syncing. And so-called secret societies are just clusters of people who figured out how to press the brain’s reward buttons without getting caught under the sofa cushions.

In this model, groups like the Sabbateans or Frankists aren’t mystical puppet-masters — they’re more like rogue neurotransmitter hacks. They looked at a system jammed with guilt signals, fear signals, inhibition signals, and basically said:

“Well, obviously the only way forward is to break every rule, confuse every authority, and see what new pathways light up. Worst case, we reinvent morality by accident.”

Which, to be fair, is sort of how biology works. When systems freeze, they improvise. Usually badly. Sometimes brilliantly.

Jiang frames it as theological drama, but if you tilt your head five degrees to the left, you can see a much simpler mechanism:
Power flows to whoever learns to route desire, anxiety, and meaning across the social synapses most efficiently.

That’s it. No demons, no Illuminati, no celestial customer service line.
Just nervous systems doing what nervous systems do, trying to keep the signal moving, even if they have to reinvent the whole species’ operating system in the process.

Jiang’s take becomes way clearer (and way funnier) once you translate it into neurotransmitter logic. And far less spooky, unless, of course, you realize we’re all still running on the same glitchy firmware.

Thanks for the enlightening lectures Prof Jiang. Whilst still on this topic, please could you look into  Golem? 

The Golem is a fascinating and powerful figure from Jewish folklore. It is an animated, anthropomorphic being, typically sculpted from clay or mud, and brought to life through mystical rituals.

The Golem of Prague
​The most famous and enduring Golem story involves Rabbi Judah Loew ben Bezalel (known as the Maharal), a prominent scholar in late 16th-century Prague.
​Purpose: Rabbi Loew is said to have created the Golem to protect the Jewish ghetto from persecution, specifically from anti-Semitic attacks and false accusations (like the "blood libel").
​The Protector: The Golem, often named Yossele, acted as a silent, super-strong, and tireless guardian, carrying out its master's orders.
​The Warning: In most versions, the Golem eventually grows violent or spirals out of control because it has no soul or morality. The Rabbi is then forced to deactivate it by removing or erasing the first letter (א, Aleph) of the word אֱמֶת (Truth), leaving מֵת (Met), which means "Dead." The clay creature then collapses back into dust.

### **@learningcurve237**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kULUM_eB8KI&lc=UgyBhKyCzI6iFic_AgR4AaABAg)

I appreciate your work and the effort you put into your content. That said, I think several claims about Islam in this video are either overstated or not well supported by the historical record, and I’d encourage a more careful framing.

On “Jews incubated Islam in Arabia”:
This framing is too reductive. Late antique Arabia involved many communities and tribal coalitions; early Islamic history includes cooperation, treaties, and also conflict with different groups (including some Jewish tribes in Medina). Reducing Islam’s emergence to “incubation” by any single community oversimplifies a complex historical setting.

On the claim “there is no evidence Muhammad existed” (mentioned in a different video):
This is a very strong claim and is difficult to sustain given the textual and material evidence. Early Qur’anic manuscripts exist with radiocarbon dating that places some parchment in the 7th century, close to the traditional period of revelation and early codification (noting that radiocarbon dates the parchment, not the ink). For example, the University of Birmingham reports radiocarbon dating of its Qur’an folios’ parchment to AD 568–645, plausibly within the earliest decades of Islam. A separate early Qur’an fragment in Tübingen has been radiocarbon dated to 649–675 CE (95% probability), again placing it within only a few decades of Muhammad’s death (traditionally 632 CE). For accuracy: radiocarbon dating does not date the ink itself.

Beyond manuscripts, the Qur’an explicitly names Muhammad and addresses his prophetic role. One clear example is Qur’an 33:40:
“Muhammad is not the father of any of your men, but he is the Messenger of Allah and the Seal of the Prophets; and Allah has full knowledge of all things.” (33:40)

Politics vs. scripture (historical point):
Yes—Muslim political history includes rulers who acted unjustly and sometimes used religion for legitimacy. But that’s an argument about political behavior, not proof that the Qur’an itself was edited to serve later regimes. The mainstream Islamic position is that the Qur’an’s wording was preserved and transmitted in a stable form.

Continuity of revelation (theological point):
If one accepts that the Torah and Gospel originated as revelation from God, then Islam’s claim follows coherently: the Qur’an is from the same God, reaffirming the core message—monotheism, accountability, and guidance—while correcting what Islam understands as later distortions in belief or transmission.

On preservation and scholarly scrutiny:
One of the most distinctive features of Islamic intellectual history is the developed tradition of isnād-based (chain-of-transmission) analysis and narrator evaluation (especially in hadith sciences). Even critics of specific reports generally acknowledge this as a systematic method of scrutiny.

Respectful disagreement:
You are absolutely free to keep your beliefs. I’m not asking you to agree with Islam—only to apply consistent standards and avoid undermining the tradition through sweeping assertions rather than careful argument.

### **@umay_yellowflower** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kULUM_eB8KI&lc=Ugz8EFZ9lYqpal_wItB4AaABAg)

The claim that Atatürk was a Dönme is historically untenable. First, Atatürk's teacher Şemsi Efendi and the father of the second Israeli president are presented as the same person. These are demonstrably two different individuals: Şemsi Efendi died in 1917 in Istanbul, where his grave remains to this day. He is documented as a reform educator who modernized the chaotic Ottoman school system, taught logic instead of dogma, and was awarded the Mecidiye Order in 1876 for his contributions. The father of the second Israeli president, Yitzhak Ben-Zvi, was born in Poland, spent 16 years in Siberian imprisonment, and died in 1953 in Jerusalem.

Second, the newspaper *Aufbau* is presented as evidence — yet all it contains is a report by Swiss orientalist Heinz Gstrein noting that some Greeks were speculating about the matter. No confirmation, no proof.

Third, it is claimed that in 1911 Atatürk told the Zionist Itamar Ben-Avi that the Jewish declaration of faith was "his prayer." This story only surfaced in 1980 — 40 years after Ben-Avi's death. More tellingly, in the original Hebrew biography published in 1943 by his stepmother, Atatürk does not appear at all — neither the bar story nor the alleged confession. It was only in 1961, at a time of political tensions between Israel and Turkey, that the biography was republished with 200 additional pages, this time including the entire Atatürk narrative.

Thank you again for expanding my horizons.
The deeper you dive into the details of our history—especially in your remarkably structured way—the more compelling it becomes.

In this lesson on Jacob Frank, I was struck by how much inner resistance I felt toward his logic. Intellectually, his arguments seemed sound, yet on an emotional level they felt deeply wrong. This contrast made the lesson particularly powerful: it illustrated how a well-constructed logical argument can still lead one astray, and how listening to one’s heart is essential to avoid falling into a devil’s trap. Contrary to the belief that this path is in any way liberal or liberating, it is in fact a near-perfect master–slave structure.

As stated in The Law of One by Ra, I do believe that those who follow Frank’s teachings may eventually experience a form of enlightenment or transcendence, precisely because they pursue service to self to its extreme. If the universe seeks to experience itself through all possible paths, then Frank’s path—like all others—must also be lived and understood.

That said, I know with certainty that this is not my path. I also believe that humanity’s collective consciousness must eventually transcend and overcome the energy-draining systems maintained by elites—or, if it fails to awaken, risk destroying itself.

Therefore, the times we are living in now present exactly this opportunity for collective evolution—emerging through the desperation and suffering of the masses. Thanks to the abundance of communication, where everyone can potentially reach everyone else, manipulation certainly exists, but so do those who touch our inner spark: those who awaken us and remind us to listen to our hearts.

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kULUM_eB8KI&lc=Ugws08KaX6P1OWoWqwx4AaABAg)

It’s both frustrating and deeply disappointing how often facts are selectively presented or quietly erased to serve a particular narrative. History is not a tool for propaganda, yet it is constantly reduced to one. When Jews were expelled from Spain in 1492, it was the Ottoman Sultan Bayezid II who opened the empire’s doors to them, even sending ships to bring them safely across the Mediterranean. Those communities did not merely survive; they flourished. In Istanbul, Jews became integral to urban life as merchants, physicians, diplomats, printers, and scholars. Ladino was spoken openly, synagogues were built, and Jewish institutions functioned under state protection for centuries.

Sabbatai Zevi, whom the rabbi later mentions, was born in Izmir, itself a product of this history of refuge. This context matters, because it shows continuity, not an isolated exception. Even in the twentieth century, when Europe descended into barbarism, that tradition did not simply vanish. During the Second World War, Turkey remained a refuge for many Jews fleeing Nazi persecution. Turkish diplomats in occupied Europe issued passports and documentation that saved thousands from deportation, and Jews in Istanbul were largely shielded from the fate of their European counterparts.

So why does the video provide an abundance of historical detail, yet completely omit these facts? Is it ignorance? Highly doubtful. Acknowledging them would complicate the narrative and disrupt the simplistic moral binaries being presented. And apparently, that is unacceptable, especially when it might force viewers to reconsider their assumptions about Muslims or Turks in a more nuanced, historically grounded way
